From Yadow Dhondeo, Deshpanday of Purgunnah Chandore.

“ Before the English reign in A. D. 1804, about 63 years ago, Jeshwuntrao Holkur, with a host of an army numbering from one to one and half lacs, crossed this en route to Poona. They looted the whole of the villages on the way. The Pindarees also plundered as they pleased. That year the monsoon was favourable and the crops excellent. But the standing crops were destroyed by Holkur’s men; granaries of corn also were looted. This caused the desertion of villages. Grain was sold at 3/4 seer per Rupee, and [p. 84] great famine was the result. The famine lasted one year. Many people subsisted on vegetables, sweet potatoes, a kind of seed called ‘Bud-bud,’ and on milk and curds.

“The second famine occurred in a. d. 1824. This was the result of the absence of rain, which prevented the growth of crops. Water became scarce, and grain was sold at enhanced rates, which the poor people had not the means of buying.

“From the ‘Kird’ belonging to Bruhmagiree Juggunnath, Deshpanday, it appears the prices ranged as follows:—

“The Mamládar of Chandore states the same rates as these are forthcoming in the Prices Currents of the village of Chandore.
“In a. d. 1804 —
“Bajree3½seers for the Rupee.
“Jowaree3½ditto.
“Rice3ditto.
“Gram3ditto.
“In a. d. 1824—
“Bajree10seers for the Rupee.
“Wheat10ditto
“Gram9½ditto
“Ooreed10ditto
“Toor11ditto

“The people who saw the famine in 1804, and who are now alive, all assert that grain was sold at ¾ seer, but there is no written record about this. Many people died that year for want of food. The number said to have died averaged about two-thirds in each village. The total number of deaths might have been between 7,000 and 9,000. Influential and liberal Sowcars tried to save lives by distributing ¼ seer grain to each man. Others made and gave a loaf to each. The Peishwa’s rule was in force, but the Peishwa did not make any arrangements for affording relief. Some state that the English Government brought jowaree and sold it in the famine-stricken districts.

“In a. d. 1824 people are not said to have died, but great distress prevailed owing to the inability of the poor to purchase grain at the enhanced prices. No relief was afforded. Merchants imported grain from cheap countries, and sold it to the inhabitants. This year remission was allowed by Government.

From Dajee Narayen, Deshpanday of Talooka Sinnur.

“There were two famines, one in a. d. 1793-94 and another in a. d. 1803-4. The famine in a. d. 1793-94 was caused by the absence of the early fall of rain, the result being that there was no Khurreef crops at all. Latterly rain fell, and Rubbee crops came on well, which helped to make [p. 85] the grain cheap. The second famine in a. d. 1803-4 was caused by the army of Holkur, who looted granaries and destroyed standing crops. Grain was sold at 3 seers for the Rupee. Muka Pindaree and Hiroo Pindaree, with their followers, also looted in that year, and committed devastations. The famine prevailed at its height from April to August 1804.

“ The prices stood as follows :—

“ In a. d. 1793-94—

“ Wheat4 seers for the Rupee.
“ Bajree5ditto.
“ Gram4ditto.
“ Rice3ditto.
“ Jowaree6ditto.
“ Dall3ditto.
“ Naglee6ditto.

“ In a. d. 1804 grain was sold at times as high as ½ seer, ¾ seer, and in some months it was 3 seers and 4 seers for the Rupee.

“The above details are given from hearsay, and not from any documents.

“Many people are said to have died. The people lived on milk and vegetable. ‘Budbudeh,’ ‘Godhdeh,’ Solé, seeds from grass, Oombri, Bhokur, and other wild fruits, &c., were brought and dried, and pounded and eaten.

From the Shreegonda Mumladar.

“1. Before the British reign, that is from the year 1396 to 1408, a great famine had occurred in the Southern Division in the Carnatic country. Kirtaram Raja offered a sacrifice to the Devee, who appeared before him and said ‘Buksh, Buksh,’ i. e. asked food. The Raja got terrified, and answered that ‘the kingdom is yours.’ The result was that for twelve years there was no rain.

“2. In 1791, 1792 and 1794, a famine was caused by rebellion.

“3. In 1804 and 1805 another famine occurred, caused by the army of Holkur rebelling.

“The information in paragraph 1 is not furnished from documents, but from tradition. It is said also that the prices were as follows. From 1396 to 1408 grain was sold 1 seer for the Rupee. In 1791 and 1792 the price was 2 pailees for the Rupee according to the Poona seer. In 1804 and 1805 grain was sold at 2 seers at first, and subsequently 10 seers. During the famine which lasted from 1396 to 1408 so many people are said to have died that for 80 miles together the villages were deserted. In 1791, [p. 86] 1792 and 1794, many people are said to have died from famine, and from cholera also in 1794. In 1804 also many people died. No arrangements were made for affording relief.

“In 1845 there was a famine on account of want of rain, when grain was sold 10 seers for the Rupee, but no people died.

“No statistics are forthcoming. The above information was obtained orally from the Deshmook and Deshpandays of the Talooka.

From the Peint Mumladar.

“The famine occurred in 1803 owing to the Holkur’s army : it lasted for about three months. Naglee was sold ½ seer for about 1½ months, and 1 seer subsequently. Some people went away to the port, and others to the Goozerat provinces. Those who remained subsisted on ‘Bud-bul ‘Koordoo,’ also on cows, buffaloes, and other animals. Some who could not get these, roasted the flesh of men and ate. By degrees after two or three years everything got cheap, and people returned and established then- selves in the villages. No measures of relief were taken. Numberless people are said to have died. There is no documentary proof. The above infcr-mation has been obtained from hearsay.

“The second famine occurred in 1824 owing to the absence of rain. It lasted for five or six months. Latterly rain fell, and everything began to prosper. During this famine Naglee was sold at 8 seers for the Rupee. A little more than 1,200 men may have died in this State during famine. People subsisted on roots, and others imported grain from above the Ghauts. No measures of relief were afforded. No documentary evidence is forth-coming.

“The third famine occurred in 1845: it lasted for six months : it was tlie result of absence of rain. Grain was sold at 3 pailees, i. e. 12 seers. There is no documentary evidence. About 1,000 men may have died. No mea-sures for affording relief were adopted.

“The fourth famine occurred in 1854 owing to the absence of rain. Above 500 men may have died for want of food. Grain was sold at 4 pailees. It was imported from above the Ghauts. No documentary proof are forthcoming. Such papers are usually burnt when huts, &c., take fire

From Meer Nuzzur Ali, wulud Moonshee Meer Ghassi, Jagheerdar, resident of Ahmednuggur.

“My grandfather, named Syed Meher Ali, who was Moonshee in the Ahmednuggur Collector’s Office, has left a manuscript volume written by him, and entitled ‘Turieek Meherr,’ in which he has noted the important occurrences in the Collectorate. The year in which this pamphlet was written cannot be ascertained. Some information regarding famines is to be found in it, which I beg to transcribe below.

[p. 87]

“A famine occurred in a. d. 1790, when grain was sold at 3 or 4 seers for the Rupee.

“A great famine occurred about a. d. 1803, when all kinds of grain were sold at one seer for the Rupee. Many people who had even ample money suffered from starvation, and many died for want of food. Some cooked and ate their children. During this famine grain was forthcoming within the fort outside the city, and it was decided by the ‘Killedar’ that people should throw earth round the fort ditch, and all who did so were daily allowed ½ seer of grain each.

“In 1824 grain was sold at 2 pailees for the Rupee.

“In 1845 grain was sold at 2 and 2½ pailees for the Rupee.”

44. Mr. Oliphant, the Acting Collector of Poona, forwarded the following reports of his Assistants on 11th December last—

From the First Assistant, Mr. Bell, dated 23rd September.

“The first instance which has been brought to my notice of suffering arising from either famine or a scarcity of grain in the Poona districts was in a. d. 1792-93, when no rain fell till October, and consequently all Khureef crops failed. The price of grain rose to 4 seers for the Rupee, and maintained that position for four months in the markets near Poona, and for twelve months in the Mawul and Joonere districts. The rain-fall in October enabled the Ryots to obtain Rubbee crops, but this was not enough to alleviate the prevailing distress, so the Peishwa’s Government brought grain from the Nizam’s country, and distributed it gratis to the sufferers There is no special mention made of a scarcity of water, nor does it appear that the scarcity of grain was so great as to deserve the name of a famine. But few people left the country, or died from want.

“2. The next instance arose from the invasion of the Deccan by Jeshwuntrao Holkur in a. d. 1802, and the consequent devastation of the country. Rain fell favourably enough for the crops, but owing to the political state of the country fields were left waste, or where.cultivated were destroyed or reaped by the contending forces. What made matters worse was that people from other districts, perhaps more severely devasted, crowded into these districts for protection and sustenance. The result was that for twelve months the price of grain stood at one seer for the Rupee. The Peishwa’s Government did what it could to relieve the starving by bringing grain from other countries and distributing it, and by remitting the land rent. Notwithstanding this, many died of starvation, and cholera superinduced by want, and many left the country in the hope of obtaining sustenance elsewhere. Again in a. d. 1824-25 no rain fell till October, and the Khureef crops consequently failed. It was a year very similar to that [p. 88] of a. d. 1792-93, though the price of grain varied only from 6 to 10 seers the Rupee during the worst months, and the difficulty of obtaining grain was considerably less. Government did not consider it necessary to take any steps to relieve those in want beyond being liberal in granting remissions of rent. That year, a. d. 1824-25, was one of scarcity only as compared with preceding and succeeding years. Compare it with the past five years during which the price of bajree has varied from 7 to 12 seers the Rupee, and it will be found that the present years press as heavily on a large class of the recipients of fixed incomes as that exceptional year pressed on the people of this district.

From the Second Assistant, Mr. East, dated 3rd October.

“1. As directed in your No. 2253, of July 9th, 1867, I have the honor to forward what information I have been able to collect with reference to previous famines and seasons of scarcity in the districts under my charge. I would observe, however, that, except in one instance, documentary evidence, dating back before the commencement of British rule, has not been attainable, and since that period, although seasons of scarcity have not been unfrequent, no famines, properly so called, are on record. Persons who were questioned as likely to be able to produce ancient manuscripts bearing on the subject stated that any they possessed had been given over to the Inam Commission, in which case they will be readily accessible to Major Etheridge.

“2. The earliest famine of which any mention is made is stated to have occurred in the year a. d. 1200, and to have lasted for twelve years, during which time great numbers of people died. Measures of relief are said to have been adopted by the Mogul Government, but owing to the lapse of time their precise nature cannot be ascertained.

“3. Another famine, but of short duration, occurred in the year a. d. 1792, grain selling at 4 to 5 seers the Rupee: it is not, however, stated that any persons actually died of hunger. In consequence of the disturbed state of the country between the years a. d. 1798 and a. d. 1805, a large quantity of land was allowed to lie fallow. Towards the end of that time the crops were seriously damaged by heavy falls of rain and hail, the result being a great scarcity of grain, which sold at 1¼ seers the Rupee. Many of the poorer classes died of hunger, but the bulk of the ryots were at that time well to do, and enabled by the sale of their effects to keep themselves from starvation. The poorer classes were assisted to a certain extent by private charity, but nothing seems to have been done by the Government.

“4. Since the commencement of British rule there have occurred, as I stated before, no actual famines. The year a. d. 1824-25 was one of [p. 89] comparative scarcity, grain selling at 10 to 12 seers the Rupee, a high price for those times, although now it would be considered cheap. Again, the year a. d. 1845-46 was a year of scarcity. In the Indapoor Talooka about Rs. 60,000 of revenue was remitted. Since then till the year a. d. 1862-63 the seasons appear to have been fairly good; but from that time till the present they have been so bad that but for the smallness of the area affected, and the improved means of communication, a famine as severe as those of former days would probably have been the result. In the year a. d. 1866-67 more than Rs. 80,000 were remitted in the Talookas of Bheemthurry and Indapoor, and upwards of Rs. 60,000 left over for collection next year. In addition, Government sanctioned a sum of Rs. 20,000 to be spent in giving employment to the poorer classes, which has been the means of alleviating to a very great extent the prevailing distress. The prospects of the present season are, I regret to say, little, if any, better than they have been for the last four years."

45. These very scanty reports were supplemented by Mr. Lloyd, Agent for Sirdars in the Deccan, with the following letters from some of the Chiefs:—

From Raja Ruttonsing Jadhowrao, Maligaonkur.

"A letter dated 1st July 1867 from Major Etheridge, Compiler of the Presidency Famine Report, having been received, you requested me in your communication No. 182, dated 22nd idem, to collect and furnish from old records, family dufturs, or from other papers in my possession, such information as might be procurable on the subject of famines and scarcities during the late and present Governments, together with the measures of relief which were then adopted, forwarding at the same time any documents throwing light on the subject. In reply to this letter I beg to state as follows.

"1. The following are the years during which failures of rain and famines occurred:—

  • “ I—Shuke 1709, a. d. 1787.
  • “ II—Shuke 1715, a. d. 1793.
  • “ III—Shuke 1726, a. d. 1804—In this year a seer of grain was sold for a Rupee.
  • “ IV—Shuke 1746, a. d. 1824—In this year 2½ pailees of grain were sold for a Rupee.
  • “ V—Shuke 1754, a. d. 1832—In this year want of rain caused the failure of crops.
[p. 90]

“ 2. The extent of the distress and its results :—

“ I—The Ryots having been subjected to many hardships in consequence of the incursions of Scindia and Holkur, at last fled the country, and left the soil uncultivated. There were many people who were rich enough to purchase grain however dear, but it was so scarce that it could not be had for money. This occurred in Shuke 1726, a. d. 1804.

“ II—In course of time the Peishwa's Government arranged for a supply of grain by importation from other countries, and abolished tolls that it might be imported free. The Peishwa also granted remissions, cowls and Tuccavees, to the Ryots. By such measures as these the people where induced to return and re-commence the cultivation of the land.

“ 3. I cannot say how far beyond the Poona districts this distress extended.

“ 4. The measures adopted for relief of such distress were usually as follow—

“ I—Brahmins were employed by the Peishwa's Government to perform certain ceremonies to propitiate the god of rain. Charities and alms were distributed. Ceremonial worship was continued to be performed even for some time after the accession of the present rule.

“ II—Whenever want of rain caused famines the Peishwa's Government granted remissions of revenue, advances and cowls, and thus protected the Ryots.

“ III—The Peishwa and his subordinate Sirdars very often employed villagers in the different departments of the State; sometimes in the Cavalry, Infantry, Elephant, and Camel branches of the service. For these duties they received remuneration. By this means the Ryot was both enabled to pay the assessment on the field which he cultivated and to find subsistence on the balance; so that he did not always stand in need of support from Government in times of scarcity.

“ The above information I give from memory and the tradition handed down from my ancestors. No old documents of a nature to furnish information on the subject are forthcoming.

From Rughoonathrao Vittul Vinchoorkur.

“ In reply to your letter No. 181, dated 22nd July 1867, desiring me to furnish from old Dufturs, family records, or other papers, information on [p. 91] the subject of past famines and scarcities in the Bombay Presidency, I beg to state as follows :—

“ I—In Shuke 1725, a. d. 1802, the Pindarees having wasted the country during a period of about three months, viz., from July to September, grain was sold as dear as ½ a seer a Rupee, though there was no lack of rain.

“ II—The poor found it very hard to maintain themselves, but as abundance of rain had fallen there was no want of vegetables. Such as could eat flesh ate flesh. Many emigrated to the Concan and Goozerat. In the latter country there were guards posted at different places by the Guicowar, so the emigrants found it even difficult to procure the common jungle fruits to subsist upon. When the Guicowar heard this he removed the guards, and allowed the emigrants to gather the fruits growing in the jungles. About the same time the Sowkars in Bombay, and the widow of Nana Fudnavis, opened free mess-houses in Bombay. The severity of the famine just before its termination was so great that mothers are said to have eaten their own children. Afterwards ‘ Tumans’ and ‘ Charuns’ imported grain into the country and relieved the sufferings of the poor.

“ III—The territory extending from the Nurbudda to Pundhurpoor was for the most part affected by the distress.

“ IV—Neither Government nor the public adopted any special measures of relief on this occasion. The strong oppressed and looted the weak with impunity.

“ The above is the only information that I have been able to gather. The old records of the family, which were deposited at the Fort of Ankaee, were destroyed on the occasion of the Government taking it. Some papers which were at Vinchoor were destroyed by fire along with the house which was burnt by Holkur.

From Krishnarao Nilkunt Moozumdar.

“ A letter dated 1st July 1867 from Major Etheridge, Compiler of the Presidency Famine Report, having been received, you requested me in your letter No. 188, dated 22nd idem, to collect and furnish from old Dufturs, family records, or other papers in my possession, or from private sources, such information as might be procurable respecting past famines and scarcities, also the measures of relief which were then adopted, forwarding, at the same time, any documents throwing light on the subject. In reply, I beg to state as follows.

“ 1. The following are the years during which failures of rain and famines occurred :—

“ I—Shuke 1709, a. d. 1787. [p. 92] “ II—Shuke 1715, a. d. 1793.

“ III—Shuke 1726, a. d. 1804—In this year a seer of grain was sold for a Rupee.

“ IV—Shuke 1746, a. d. 1824—In this year 2½ pailees of grain were sold for a Rupee.

“ V—Shuke 1754, a. d. 1832—In this year want of rain caused the failure of crops, and in some instances the Ryots plundered shops.

“ 2. The extent of the distress and its results.

“ I—The disturbances caused by the invasion of Scindia and Holkur, and the hardships which followed thereupon to the Ryots, made them leave the country, and so the soil remained uncultivated. Grain was so scarce that it could not be had for money even by rich people. This occurred in Shuke 1726, a. d. 1804.

“ II—Subsequently the Peishwa's Government arranged for supplying the Ryots with grain from other countries, abolished tolls in order to its free importation, and granted remissions of revenue, cowls and advances. By these means people were induced to return and undertake the cultivation of the land.

“ 3. It is not known how far the distress extended. So much is known that it affected the Poona districts.

“ 4. The measures adopted for relief were as follow :—

“ I—The Peishwa's Government employed Brahmins to perform propitiatory worship to the god of rain, and distributed charities and alms. These ceremonies were continued even for some years after the introduction of the present rule.

“ II—Whenever want of rain caused distress, the Peishwa's Government granted remissions, advances and cowls, and thus protected the Ryots.

“ I write this from memory and family tradition. No old documents of a nature to furnish information are in my possession.

From Rungrao Venayeh, Poorundhuray.

“ I received your letter No. 185, dated 22nd July 1867, in which, on a letter dated 1st idem from Major Etheridge, Compiler of the Presidency Famine Report, you desire me to furnish information respecting the times and extents of past famines and scarcities; in reply to which I beg to state as follows :—

“ I—There was a great famine in Shuke 1724, a. d. 1802, which is known as the year of disturbance of Holkur. The second famine [p. 93] occcurred in Fuslee 1234, A. D. 1824, which is called the year of the ‘Khurpud.’ And the third famine has continued from Fuslee 1272, A. D. 1862, up to the present time, and is styled the current dearness of grain.

“A.—The first famine, that of Shuke 1724, A. D. 1802, resulted from the invasion of Holkur. There was then such a scarcity of grain that it was not procurable for money. Thousands of people died of starvation. The consequence was that the district bore the aspect of a desert.

“B.—The second famine, that of Shuke 1746, A. D. 1824, the year of the ‘Khurpud,’ arose from a general failure of rain. Grain, though old and rotten, was sold at 10 seers a Rupee. Epidemics broke out. There was universal sickness. Some people fled the country. Cattle died from want of grass.

“C.—The third famine, the present dearness of grain, is not so severe as those above mentioned. It owes its origin partly to the failure of rain, and partly to the taxes imposed on communities who are barely able to support themselves.

“II—The first famine affected almost the whole of the Deccan. The second, that of Shuke 1746, A. D. 1824, the districts of Poona, Nuggur, Candeish, and Sholapoor. The third still prevails in all parts of India.

“III—The distress occasioned by the first famine was mitigated by the importation of grain from other countries. Government also reduced the assessment on land, and encouraged and assisted traders to import grain freely.

“IV—The distress caused by the second famine, that of Shuke 1746, A. D. 1824, was lessened by an opportune fall of rain on the day of ‘Dusera,’ and good crops in some districts. Subsequently harvests became more flourishing.

“V—The distress caused by the third famine is still prevailing. It rains not as required. Servants, labourers, &c., have to pay very high prices for the necessaries of life.

“The above information is stated from what I have heard and gathered from other people. No documentary evidence of a nature to throw light on the subject is with me.

From Munohur Bheorao, Khasnavis and Potnis.

“In reply to your letters Nos. 183 and 214, dated 24th August 1867, desiring me to collect and furnish from old records, family dufturs and [p. 94] other papers in my possession, or from other sources, information on the subject of the famines and scarcities which have occurred during the late and present Governments, the extent of the distress occasioned, the area of the country affected, and the measures of relief adopted, I beg to state that no documentary evidence of a nature to throw light on this subject is in my possession, but from hearsay and family tradition I am able to say as follows:—

“I—In Shuke 1714, a. d. 1792, a famine occurred, during which dearness of grain prevailed to such an extent that a pailee of grain was sold for a Rupee in the Poona districts. In Shuke 1726, a. d. 1804, a second famine occurred, during which one seer of grain was sold for a Rupee. These two famines resulted from failure of rain.

“II—The distress extended over the southern part of the Poona districts.

“III—For the relief of the distress the Peishwa’s Government employed Brahmins to perform the usual ceremonies to propitiate the Almighty to send down rain, distributed charities and alms, established free mess-houses, granted advances and cowls, and, after inspection of crops, allowed remission of assessment. Tolls were also abolished in order to the free importation of grain, and traders were encouraged to bring it from other countries. By these means the Ryots were protected.

“IV—After the introduction of the British rule, owing to the failure of rain in Shuke 1746, a. d. 1824, grain was sold from 2 to 2½ pailees the Rupee. This distress continued up to Ashween Shood 10th, on which day rain fell and produced good crops. In Shuke 1754, a. d. 1832, the grain-sellers raised the price of grain, but Government issued orders and stopped the evil.

“The above is all the information I have been able to collect.

From Nilkuntrao Mahadeo Poorundhuray.

“In reply to your letter No. 187, dated 2nd July 1867, desiring me to furnish from old dufturs, family records, or other papers in my possession, or from private sources, information respecting past famines and scarcities, together with the measures of relief adopted, forwarding at the same time any documents throwing light on the subject, I regret to state that I am not in a position to comply, having in my possession no documentary evidence, and not deeming tradition or hearsay reliable.

From Kesheorao Ramchundur Jog.

“In reply to your letter of 22nd July 1867, desiring me to furnish [p. 95] information regarding famines and scarcities prior to and since the introduction of the British rule, I beg to state as follows :—

" I—During the Peishwa’s rule, in a. d. 1803-4, the armies of Scindia and Holkur came to Poona and fought. Plunder to a great extent was the consequence. There was also a drought, and the crops suffered. Grain was sold from a quarter of a seer to one seer for a Rupee, and even at this rate could be procured with difficulty ; consequently great numbers of people died of starvation. I know nothing regarding the measures adopted for the relief of the distress, as I was very young. I am unable to procure any documents throwing light on the subject, nor can I state precisely the extent of the country affected, nor the period during which the famine lasted.

" II—After the introduction of the British rule a drought occurred in a. d. 1824, and grain was not sown in sufficient quantities. It consequently became dear. To alleviate the distress Government commenced the construction of the Koorkoomb and Babdeo Ghauts, and thus gave employment to sufferers. The scarcity of grain in those days, however, was not greater than that which has prevailed during the last four years. Fodder was not so dear as it is now. It is said that the scarcity affected an area of about 100 miles round Poona.

From Wamunrao Mahadeo, Raheereckur.

" I received your letter No. 186, dated 22nd July last, desiring me to furnish information regarding famines and scarcities, and in reply I beg to state as follows :—

During the late Rule.

" In Fuslee 1201-2, a. d. 1791-92, grain was sold at from 6 to 7 seers for a Rupee in the districts round Poona and in the Concan. Although there was a sufficient fall of rain, and consequent good harvests, still a scarcity arose from the bringing together of the troops intended for the expedition of Khurda. When this was over the country again became prosperous. People did not suffer much from this distress.

" In Fuslee 1213-14, a. d. 1803-4, a Rupee would buy only 1½ or 1¾ seers of grain. This dearness lasted for three or four months. It arose from the incursion of Scindia and Holkur. Throughout the districts of Khandeish, Nuggur and Poona, where it rained, and there were consequent good crops, they were laid waste by the armies of those Chiefs. Other districts were in a flourishing state. When the disturbance ceased the prices of grain began to decline. For the relief of this distress Government granted remissions of revenue, and abolished the customs. Rich people [p. 96] brought grain from other countries, and supplied the poor in charity. Government also employed Brahmins to propitiate the deity to send down rain and bless the sufferers.

During the present Rule.

“Fuslee 1233-4, a. d. 1823-24, in which year there was a famine, was known as the year of the ‘Khurpud.’ It rained at the commencement of the monsoon, but no rain fell after. On the day of ‘Dusera’ much rain fell, and Ryots who had grain at once sowed it, but no crops resulted. The price of grain rose very high. This distress lasted for the whole year. Government granted Ryots lands in farm, some free from assessment and some on Mamool assessment, and relieved them from distress in this manner.

“In Fuslee 1242-3, a. d. 1832-33, there was a famine, which owed its origin to failure of rain, and high prices resulted.

“Since Fuslee 1273, a. d. 1863, up to the present time the price of grain has continued high. People earn their livelihood with great difficulty. Government partially relieved this distress by granting to their servants compensation on account of high prices, and by sanctioning works of public utility in the Engineering and Railway Departments. Cultivators are enabled to support themselves, and pay Government with ease the light assessment on their fields, from the produce of their crops in good seasons. Moreover, Government have imposed a new tax, and as the poor are unable to work this tax acts as a bar to trade. The Sowkars have become bankrupt. These causes continue to make people pass their days with difficulty.”

46. Mr. Arthur, the Collector of Sattara, gives very little information, and regrets that “the entire absence of records makes it impossible to supply more.” That gentleman writes on 17th January last as follows:—

“1. With reference to your Circular of the 1st July last, I have the honor to inform you that no information can be obtained in this district regarding any famines which may have occurred in years beyond the memory of men now living. All the old papers in the possession of families of note in this part of the country were taken away by the Officers of the Inam Commission, and are for the most part, I believe, recorded in your Office.

“2. The famine years regarding which oral information is forthcoming are a. d. 1792, 1803 and 1824.

“3. In the first of these years the famine appears to have raged throughout the Sattara territory. It was occasioned partly by the scanty [p. 97] fall of rain, and partly by the political troubles which then prevailed. It lasted, it seems, about a year, during which time grain was sold for the most part at 3 seers per Rupee.

“4. The Peishwa’s Government is said to have then endeavoured, as far as possible, to relieve the distress which was so generally felt throughout the Deccan, but I cannot find that any public works of importance in the Sattara territory had their origin in his liberality.

“5. The dearth of a. d. 1803 does not appear to have had its origin in a failure of crops in this part of the country: in the Nizam’s country, and also in Goozerat, there appears to have been a great failure of crops, owing to which people flocked in great numbers from distant parts to Sattara, and the result was to create a famine similar to that which prevailed elsewhere. This famine is said to have reached its height in the months of July and August, and stories up to this time are told, with horror, of men having been reduced to the extremity of subsisting on human flesh. Corn is said to have been sold at one seer per Rupee, and so many as 25,000 strangers are said to have flocked into the town of Waee to obtain what they could from the liberality of the Punt Pritinidhi, the Rastia, and such like wealthy persons. It is stated that no fewer than 10,000 persons died in Waee during this famine.

“6. The dearth of a. d. 1824 does not appear to have been so severe, and perhaps was more local in its nature. Grain was then sold, it is said, at about 6 seers for a Rupee, and people emigrated for food both in the direction of Nuggur and of Kolapoor.

“7. I regret that I can only give this general account regarding what has occurred in this district, but the entire absence of records makes it impossible to supply more detailed information.”

47. From Sholapoor the Sub-Collector, Mr. G. W. Anderson, reported on 6th September that there were no official records which gave any account of past famines, and that he could do no more than forward what information the Mumludars had been able to obtain from private sources. The several Mumludars and the District Deputy Collector wrote as follows:—

Sholapoor Mamludar.

“ Since the introduction of the British rule, in Fuslee 1227, a. d. 1817, a great famine, called ‘Kurdee famine,’ occurred in Fuslee year 1228, a. d. 1818, when people lived by eating ‘Kurdee.’ Two famines afterwards raged in Fuslee years 1241-42 and 1264-65, a. d. 1831-32 and 1854-55, owing to want of rain, and they extended over the whole of the Talooka. [p. 98] It does not appear that Government afforded any other means of relief to the famine-stricken people except by employing them on works of public utility. The merchants also lent their aid to the distressed by distributing grain and food to them. Before the commencement of the British rule the rate of grain was 1¼ seer for the Rupee, and the Peishwa's Government distributed food to the sufferers.

Barsee Mamludar.

“The great Doorga Deevee famine took place in A. D. 1396, and spread over the whole of Hindostan. Rain fell slightly in A. D. 1794, when there was a war carried on between the Peishwa and the Nizam. Much grain was then plundered, and the standing crops were destroyed by armies, &c., traversing the country. Grain was then selling at 1½ seers for the Rupee. The Peishwa's Government did not give any relief to the sufferers. In Fuslee year 1213, A. D. 1803, the rate of grain was still higher. It was sold at 1¼ seer for the Rupee, owing to no rain having fallen. The Peishwa and the Nimbalkur protected the people from the scourge of famine by giving them food and clothes. In A. D. 1824-25, too, there was a famine owing to the scarcity of rain. There was a failure of crops. Ten bundles of Kurbee were sold for the Rupee. Many cattle died for the want of Kurbee. This famine extended over the whole of the Talooka. Government were pleased to remit assessment on the lands in consequence. In A. D. 1832-33 and 1845-46, owing to rain not having fallen seasonably, grain was sold at 8 seers for the Rupee. Government were pleased in these years also to make remissions of assessment.

Marah Mamludar.

“In Fuslee year 1202-3, A. D. 1792-3, the rate of grain was 5 seers for the Rupee, owing to the marauding incursions of Scindia and Holkur. No information is forthcoming as to the measures, if any, adopted for the relief of the poor. The famine extended over 200 ‘koss.’ The rate of grain in Fuslee year 1213, A. D. 1803, was 1¼ seer for the Rupee, owing partly to its having been plundered by rebels, and partly to its scarcity. Many persons died of starvation. In A. D. 1824-25, 1832-33, 1845-46 and 1854-55, arose a general outcry of distress from want of rain, when, by way of relief, some works were carried on.

Kurmalla Mamludar.

“In Fuslee year 1204, A. D. 1794, in consequence of no rain having fallen, and because of the war between the Nizam and the Peishwa, grain was sold at 5 seers for the Rupee.

[p. 99]

“In Fuslee year 1214, a. d. 1804, because of the absence of rain and of the devastation caused by the forces of Scindia and the Pindarees, grain was sold at 1¾ or 2 seers for the Rupee. Many persons died of starvation. The Nimbalkur afforded relief to the sufferers by distributing food.

“In a. d. 1824-25 there was a great scarcity of grain: it was sold at 10 seers for the Rupee. The scarcity extended over about 200 miles.

The District Deputy Collector.

“1. I have the honor to acknowledge the receipt of your endorsement No. 1440, of the 12th July last, forwarding copy of a letter from Major Etheridge, and requesting to be furnished with information regarding past famines in the Talookas in my charge.

“2. Immediately on the receipt of your orders, I addressed the Mumladars, and asked them to use every endeavour to obtain full information from every available source, and especially to take copies of all documentary evidence bearing on the subject.

* Of Punderpoor and Sangola.

“3. From the report of Mumladars,* which I enclose, it will be noticed that they have not been able to obtain any documents, owing to the Officers. of the Inam Commission having removed all old records from the districts. The information embodied in the reports has been obtained by oral enquiries among old inhabitants. The reports give full details. I shall annex a succinct account of the more important features.

“4. The oldest famine about which information can be obtained is the one that is said to have occurred about a. d. 1396, and to have lasted 12 years. It is known by the name of the ‘Doorga Devee’ famine. It extended over the whole of India south of the Nurbudda, and depopulated whole districts. It arose from the total want of seasonable rain. It cannot be ascertained whether any measures of relief were adopted at the time.

“5. The next in order is the famine said to have occurred about the year a. d. 1460, and the memory of which has been perpetuated by a poetical legend very popular in the Deccan, and known as ‘Damajee Punt’s Akhyan’ (history). Damajee, the legend states, was a Brahmin, and employed at Mungul Vedh, about 12 miles from Punderpoor, as an Officer of revenue under the Mogul Government. He had collected a large quantity of corn at Mungul Vedh on account of the Government. Owing to a famine which broke out, hundreds of Brahmins and others flocked to Mungul Vedh, and were fed by Damajee out of the Government stores. The Mogul Emperor was wrath, and issued orders that Damajee should be arrested and carried to his presence to receive merited punishment. While Damajee was being thus led to suffer for doing an act of justifiable benevolence, [p. 100] Vittoba, the great Idol of Punderpoor, and the patron divinity of Damajee, took compassion upon his devoted servant, and, assuming the garb of a village Mhar, went to the great Emperor's court, and paid in cash for the whole of the corn distributed by Damajee. Such is an outline of this legend, which possesses some merit as a poetical composition, and is the production of a mind impressed by a deep feeling of devotion. At Mungul Vedh the village priests point out the alleged site of Damajee's house and of the corn-cellars. This famine, which also was the result of want of rain, lasted for a year, and is said to have been felt in the Deccan and Southern India.

“6. A famine of equal extent and duration to that described in the last paragraph is said to have occurred about the year 1520. This is said to have been caused by successions of Military hordes destroying the crops and plundering every thing that came in their way. No measures of relief were adopted.

“7. The price at which grain used to sell during the famines mentioned above has not been ascertained.

“8. A. D. 1791 was another year of famine. The distress in the Carnatic was excessive, as the crops there had entirely failed. In the Deccan about half, or a fourth, of the usual yield was obtained, but in consequence of thousands having come from the Carnatic to the Deccan for support, the distress throughout was great. Thefts became the order of the day, and large numbers fell victims to the scourge. The rigor of the calamity lasted about four months. Nothing appears to have been done to relieve the distress. Corn sold at 3 seers per Rupee.

“9. In A. D. 1802 the country was the scene of perpetual raids and plunder. Scindia's armies were in the field. Hordes of freebooters also carried fire and sword everywhere. The crops were destroyed, and large numbers of people were killed. The consequence of this state of things was naturally a famine, which extended over the Deccan, Candeish and Southern India. A large number of persons is said to .have died for want of food. Dhondbhut Kutkey, an inhabitant of Punderpoor, is said to have fed many at that town; and an officer of the Nimbulkur's, stationed at Mohole, is also reputed to have given food to many for several days. There are no means of ascertaining the extent of these private charities. The intensity of the famine was felt during three months, and at the height of the visitation corn sold as dear as ¾ seer per Rupee.

“10. There was a partial famine in the districts of Punderpoor, Barsee, Tooljapoor and the surrounding country, in 1818, partly on account of the ravages made by Bajeerow's armies, and partly by the total failure of crops towards Baleghat. A Sowcar of Vyrog in the Barsee Talooka, [p. 101] surnamed Ghongday, is said to have given food to all who resorted to him for it. How many persons were thus succoured by him cannot be ascertained. Cholera of a fearful type also broke out this year, sending thousands to an untimely grave. It may be remarked en passant that this was the first recognized appearance of Cholera on this side of India. Grain sold at 3 to 4 seers per Rupee during the height of the famine.

“11. In 1824 was also experienced a partial famine in the Punderpoor and surrounding districts from the Bheema to the Godavery. This was caused by want of seasonable rain. Scindia had kept an ‘Anchutra’ at Punderpoor, and the Ghongdays of Vyrog had their Chutra also, but for the relief of the large majority of sufferers no organized arrangements were made. This scarcity lasted for a year. Corn sold at 6 to 8 seers a Rupee.

“12. The last famine remaining to be noticed is that which occurred in 1832. This was also caused by want of seasonable rain, and prevailed in the Sholapoor and adjoining provinces, embracing an area of nearly 150 square miles. Many left their lands and homes. Government had to grant remissions. Maharoodra Bapoo Kulowdeb, a Brahmin Sowcar, and many Marwaree Sowcars, are said to have fed thousands for several weeks at Sholapoor. The Ghongdays of Vyrog acted with their usual generosity. Public works were also commenced by Government to provide work for the helpless. The famine lasted nine or ten months, and corn used to sell at 5 to 6 seers a Rupee.”

48. The Political Agent at Kolapoor, Lieutenant Colonel G. S. Anderson, also reported on 26th November that no records of famines in the Kolapoor districts prior to the present century were forthcoming; and added that—

“3. These districts being situated near the Ghauts, and having generally a good rain-fall, do not appear within the present century to have suffered from any severe famine caused by a local failure of crops. Occasionally the seasons have been bad, and prices have been unusually high, but there has been no year which could be styled a famine year, simply as regards the tract of country embraced in the Kolapoor territory. In 1804-5, however, although the season was a tolerably good one in Kolapoor itself, all the effects of a famine were experienced from the large number of starving emigrants coming from other districts. The causes of the scarcity in more remote districts can best be gleaned from information obtained from the British Collectorates. The main cause assigned by the Kolapoor Officials is the ruthless course of devastation to which the Mahratta armies subjected the country in the interior. Holkur’s wanton destruction of the crops and grain supplies of the Godavery districts is especially [p. 102] referred to. Kolapoor then became a refuge for many destitute persons, and the consequent pressure on the local supplies caused the price of grain to rise to 2 or 1½ seers of different kinds of grain per Rupee. A good many deaths from starvation appeared to have occurred, but there is no reliable information from which even an approximate estimate of their number could be given. The ruling Raja of Kolapoor, Sivajee, by all accounts, did his best to mitigate the calamity by the distribution of boiled food to the starving people who had flocked to Kolapoor from other quarters. The Himmut Bahader, the Bheem Bahader, and other Sirdars, and well-to-do persons, are said to have followed the good example set by the Raja. Luckily the following year was one of great abundance, which enabled the effects of this severe distress to be quickly recovered from.

“4. As above mentioned, there have been other years of scarcity and high prices, but they do not appear to require special mention as famine years.

“5. I regret the meagreness of this statement, but there is always a difficulty in getting full or reliable information from the records of these Native States. Moreover, Kolapoor, from its situation, has happily been more exempt from famine than most Indian districts, and thus does not afford much matter for report.”

49. The Collector of Belgaum, Mr. Grey, on 18th February last stated that the information forthcoming regarding past famines in that district also was “extremely scanty,” that his endeavours had entirely failed to bring forth any thing of any value, and that he could do nothing more than quote oral tradition. Mr. Grey wrote as follows:—

“2. The information forthcoming regarding past famines in this district is extremely scanty. Previous to the introduction of the British rule no record of such occurrences appears to have been kept, and references to the Mumladars to procure such Native records and papers that may be in the possession of the hereditary district and village officers have entirely failed to bring forth any thing of any value. Such information as is procurable rests entirely on oral tradition, and from this it may be gathered that throughout the Southern Mahratta Country, in consequence of excessive drought and swarms of locusts, there was a gradual failure of crops, which commenced from Fuslee year 1197, a. d. 1787-88, and continued to a. d. 1795-96, and caused great distress among all classes. The famine appears to have been at its height about 1791-92, and it having extended to the country bordering on the Godavery the people from those parts flocked in numbers into this province, thereby raising the price of food to such an [p. 103] extent that it was scarcely procurable. It is said that some of the higher classes being unable to obtain grain at any price, and rejecting animal food, poisoned themselves, while the poorer classes found a scanty subsistence from roots, herbs, dead animals, and even human corpses. Altogether 25,000 persons are said to have died from starvation in the town and district of Gokak alone. There is a tradition that a woman in Gokak was driven by hunger to devour her own offspring, but that the indignation of the late Government was so great that she was immediately put to death by being tied to the feet of a buffaloe, and thus trodden to death.

“3. It does not appear that any specific measures of relief were set on foot by the late Government. Private charity was exercised by the Jagheerdars to the extent of their ability, but the country being entirely land-locked there was no communication with other districts, and after the supplies in store were exhausted grain was not obtainable by any classes.

“4. The next famine appears to have occurred in the years Fuslee 1212 to 1214, a. d. 1802-03 to 1804-5, and was caused by excessive drought by immigration of people from the Godavery districts and the march of the Peishwa's army through the country, which in many parts quite put a stop to cultivation. The people suffered nearly as much from distress in these years as they did from the famine which prevailed in 1787-88, and no organized measures of relief appear to have been set on foot by the late Government. It is said that 15,000 persons died in the Gokak district alone, but there is no record whatever obtainable regarding this famine.

“5. There appears to have been great scarcity in the Southern Mahratta Country in the year 1832, and prices were very high, but the distress cannot be said to have amounted to a famine; and although some people are said to have sold their children to obtain food, there is no reliable information obtainable that any deaths occurred. Belgaum was not formed into a separate Collectorate till the year 1836, so the records of this year are all with the Collector of Dharwar, and I have not had an opportunity of referring to them.

“6. I regret that the information forthcoming on this subject is so meagre; but if you can point out any sources in this district from which you consider that any reliable history can be derived, I shall be happy to make further enquiries.”

50. Under these circumstances it would simply have been waste of time to suggest that further minute enquiries should be made, for eight months had already been occupied in procuring the above meagre account.

[p. 104]

51. Nor was more ample information forthcoming from the Kulladghee Collectorate. Mr. Armstrong in like manner, after eight months' search and labour, reported as follows on 21st February last.

"1. With reference to your Circular of 1st July last, I have the honor to report that the famines that occurred in the Talookas now forming the Kulladghee district are as follows :—

Before the introduction of the British Rule.

"2. In Fuslee 1201, A. D. 1791, there was a famine owing to want of rain, and no stores of grain to fall back on. No measures of relief were taken, and the people appear to have been much harassed by the marching of troops. There were very many deaths from want of food. This year was called 'Dogee Burra,' two Canarese words, 'Dogee,' skull, and 'Burra,' a famine. The interpretation given to me is that people died in such number from hunger that they could not be buried.

"3. In Fuslee 1213, A. D. 1803, the rain-fall was good, and so were the crops, but owing to the Pindarees there was a famine. Grain sold from 2 to 4 Bengal seers the Rupee, and in the Beejapoor Talooka as high as 1½ seers the Rupee. No measures for relief were taken, but in the Bagulcote 'Prant' some of the merchants fed the starving. The distress appears to have been great, and people died from want. This year was called 'Ragee Burra' famine, in which people ate 'Ragee.'

"4. As regards these two famines no documents are procurable from the Zemindars, or other hereditary officers. The information obtained has been from what people have heard one from the other.

British Rule.

"5. In 1818—20 there was a famine from want of rain in the Talookas of Moodebehal, Hypurga, Indee, Beejapoor, and Mungolee, which lasted from six to nine months. In the Indee Talooka there was something of a crop, but it was soon eaten up, as people in quest of food crowded in from the Nizam's territories. 'Jowaree' was sold at from 6 to 8 Bengal seers a Rupee. A considerable number of people and cattle are said to have died from starvation. People were reduced to eating tamarind seeds parched in the fire. There is nothing to show that any measures for relief were taken to alleviate the distress.

"6. In 1824-25 in the Talookas of Indee, Moodebehal, Mungolee and Hypurga, there was a famine owing to want of rain. 'Jowaree' sold at 16 Bengal seers the Rupee. No deaths from want appear to have taken place. There is nothing to show that any measures were taken to relieve the distress.

[p. 105]

"7. In 1832-33 there was a failure of crop for want of rain throughout the whole Talookas of the district, viz., Indee, Hypurga, Moodebehal, Mungolee, Beejapoor, Bagulcote, Hoongoond, and Badamee. 'Jowaree' sold at 8 Bengal seers the Rupee. Mr. Arbuthnot, the Sub-Collector at Bagulcote, distributed food to the people gratis in the Indee and Moodebehal, Mungolee and Hypurga Talookas, and also employed the people in making roads. Many deaths are said to have been caused by starvation, and the mortality among cattle to have been great.

"8. In 1853-54 there was a crop throughout the district, with the exception of the Indee Talooka, where it failed. Owing to a failure of crop in the Sholapoor State, the people emigrated from there. Prices rose, and 'Jowaree' sold in the Indee Talooka at 7 Bengal seers the Rupee. In the other Talookas it sold at 25 seers the Rupee. No deaths are reported to have occurred from want. Owing to the high prices of grain the people were employed in making roads and on other works in the Talookas of Indee and Hypurga.

"9. From 1863-64 to 1866-67 the crops have not been good from want of rain, but owing to the people being well off, and to large supplies of grain from Sholapoor, distress was but little felt. In 1865-66 the sum of Rs. 10,000 was sanctioned for works on which to employ the people."

52. From Dharwar Mr. Reid, the Collector, reported on 19th February that he had not been able to find out any other particulars of past famines than those recorded in the following statements by his subordinates :—

By Mr. King, First Assistant Collector.

"The information which I have been able to obtain is very meagre, and regarding the earlier famines the records are silent. The most destructive famine is the earliest of which I have any account, and appears to have taken place in 1791—93, but I cannot give the date with accuracy. It is known by the picturesque name of the 'Dogi Bura,' or Golgotha famine. It is still remembered in the districts of Hooblee, Dumbul and Kulghutgee. The people were reduced to feeding on leaves and berries, and women and children were sold. From Dumbul I am informed that the rain-fall failed for twelve years, and that for three there was no cultivation. The Mysore Government, to which these districts were subject, appears to have seized the stores of grain in Hooblee, and the rich are said to have come forward to the assistance of their poorer brethren. One seer of grain of only half the capacity of the present seer* sold for 10 annas and 8 pies in Dumbul. The next famine of note appears to have taken place about sixty-three years ago, but I cannot furnish [p. 106] the date exactly. The Deshpandey of Seegaum remembers it, and he tells me that it lasted for a year, and was caused by a great immigration of starving people from the country about Punderpoor and Bejapoor. It is still known in the Hooblee district as the ‘Bayan Bura,’ or ‘Terrible’ famine. Grain seems to have been sold at 5 or 6 seers a Rupee, and the wealthy supplemented the wants of the poorer classes, being headed in Hooblee by Chintamonrao Putwurdhun. It appears to have extended through all my districts, and I cannot find that any steps were taken for its amelioration by the Native Government. In 1832, in consequence of a failure of rain and an immigration from districts beyond the Krishna, great scarcity was experienced in all the districts. The price of grain varied from 7 seers in Hooblee to 9 in Dumbul, but in the latter Talooka some of the poorest class were reduced to eating grass. The rich in Hooblee, headed by Appoorao Lokhundy, subscribed for the relief of the poor, and Government remitted the tolls. Mr. Elliot, the Collector, also issued an order forbidding forestalling and regrating, and requiring the dealers to bring their grain into the market. Tanks and other works of utility were undertaken to provide labour for the poor. The three famines noticed above appear to have been general. From Dumbul I hear of two minor famines; one in 1814 was called the ‘Bisaghee Bura,’ a name the meaning of which I have not been able to discover. It was apparently caused by cultivation being stopped owing to the disturbed state of the country. It only lasted two months, during which grain was sold at 7 seers the Rupee. I imagine that it cannot have been confined to Dumbul only, but its limits are not given. In 1824 another short famine is recorded, also lasting two months, in which grain was sold at 12 seers the Rupee. It is attributed to a failure in the rain supply, and appears to have extended into the Belgaum districts. I have not sketched the famine of last year, as the reports made by the Collector to Government contain all the information regarding it required by Major Etheridge.

By the Deputy Collector.

“ 1. With reference to your endorsement No. 1253, dated 10th July last, calling for information as to past famines in this district, I beg to report that, though considerable search has been made among the old records, I have been unable to discover any information on the subject.

“ 2. I beg, however, to append a memorandum forwarded to me by Ramrow Bhimajee, late Akburnavees of Kolapoor, descriptive of a famine which occurred in the districts lying east of Dharwar about sixty-six or sixty-seven years ago. A translation of the memorandum is annexed.

“ 3. As a report on the famine which appeared in the Dharwar districts in 1866 has been already laid by you before Government, it will, I presume, be unnecessary for me to make any report thereon.

[p. 107]

By Ramrao Bhimajee, late Akburnavees at Kolapoor.

" 1. With reference to your order for information regarding past famines in the Carnatic, I beg to report that about sixty-six or sixty-seven years ago, owing to drought and failure of crops, the inhabitants of Dousall in the Beejapoor districts and the surrounding villages swarmed into this part of the country—I mean Dharwar, &c. On their way they could get neither food to eat nor water to drink. They arrived at Dharwar in a most deplorable condition, and, finding no shelter and food here, laid themselves down amongst the bushes of Shegeekaee (a prickly plant, which then grew around the fort), and numbers of them died for want of food.

" 2. At that time this country was blessed with good harvest seasons and rain. Information of the expected arrival of foreigners reached the community of Dharwar about two years before. At this time the insurrection headed by Donjibag occurred, and provisions became scarce. Those who had grain to spare did not venture to assist the sufferers, under the impression that they would have to share the same fate in case of the famine extending to Dharwar.

" 3. How far the famine had spread in the Beejapoor district could not be traced, but this district was free from it.

" 4. No measures were taken by the Government of the day to relieve the sufferers, nor was any assistance afforded to such of them as had repaired to this part in hope of getting food. It was then the Peishwa’s Government.

" 5. Of the above no documentary evidence is forthcoming, but it can be traced that this famine was called ‘ Dogi Bara,’ as the sufferers died striking their heads against each other. ‘ Dogi’ is a Canarese word signifying skull. Another reason for styling it ‘ Dogi Bura’ is that most of the inhabitants above mentioned died on the way. Thousands of dead bodies were to be seen lying on the highways. The bodies, after lying for some time, were removed and buried by ‘ Mahars.’ This famine is also called ‘ Byan Bura,’ because those who came to this part of the country begged food on the way-side villages in a piteous tone, and when they got no assistance they laid down and died.’

53. Mr. Reid’s report on the famine of 1866 is given in full below :—

" 1. The distress in the Dharwar districts reached its height in the months of August, September and October 1866. The Talookas in which the greatest destitution appeared were Nuwulgoond, Roan and Dumbul, eastward of the station of Dharwar.

[p. 108]

"2. The distress may be stated as the result of several successive bad seasons. Though the monsoon of 1866 in Dharwar set in late, a fair harvest was looked for until August, when, owing to the rains keeping off, grain had not only become dear, but scarce in the market, and most distressing accounts of the condition into which the poorer classes were plunged continued to reach the Collector throughout August and September. Very little rain had fallen throughout the districts up to the end of September, when heavy and continued falls saved the crops.

"3. With a view to afford relief to the sufferers, and to provide work for the unemployed, beneficial public works, for which skilled labour was not necessary, were commenced in the Talookas of Dharwar, Dumbul, Roan and Nuwulgoond. A special grant of 40,000 Rupees was made by Government from Local Funds, and Rupees 3,500 from Imperial Funds for the purpose. A special grant of 50,000 Rupees was also given for the improvement of the high road from Teygoor via Dharwar to Hurreeher, and an advance of Rs. 16,000 promised, if necessary, to the Municipality of Dharwar for the improvement of a large tank in the suburbs of the town.

"4. Considerable numbers of the poor thus found employment for several months until the harvest was gathered, which the seasonable late rains of September and October saved.

"5. The following extracts from reports made to the Collector in September by the First Assistant Collector and District Deputy Collector respectively of this district show the extent to which the distress prevailed.

Extract paragraphs 3, 4 and 5, of Mr. Hope's report No. 45, dated 15th September 1866.

"" 3. The distress existing in the Dumbul Talooka is the result not of one year's bad harvest, but of a continual failure of crops for some three or four years past. Even now, however, it is owing (at least at Gudug) not so much to actual scarcity of food as to the dearness of food in consequence of apprehensions as to the approaching harvest. The Ryots who have grain stored are naturally reluctant to part with it lest they should afterwards come to stand in need of it. Rain has, up to the present date, been very deficient in this Talooka. There was a little a month or so ago, barely sufficient to enable the cultivators to sow some of their fields, but a large portion of the country is lying waste, and most of the crops in those fields which have been sown are being blighted for want of moisture. A sufficient [p. 109] supply of rain is by no means yet despaired of (it is known to have fallen lately in the surrounding country); and if it comes it cannot but have a most beneficial effect upon the grain market.

“ ‘4. The gloomy prospects of the season are felt greatly by the people. The cultivators are prevented from following their usual pursuits, many people have been reduced to beggary, and still more have left their homes and gone to seek a livehood elsewhere. The town of Dambal has been deserted by its inhabitants to a great extent, and presents a very melancholy appearance. Many of the deserted homes have been left carelessly open, but most of them have been shut up, indicating an intention on the part of the people to return to them when better times come.

“ ‘5. Both in Gudug and in Dambal steps have been taken for the feeding of those who are utterly destitute and incapacitated from work. This is, of course, a much larger class at Gudug (including Bettigherry) than at Dambal. Subscriptions have been raised at both places sufficient to preserve this class from starvation for some time to come. The emergency has called forth highly commendable munificence on the part of some members of the wealthier Native community, especially at Gudug, where one individual (Muddiwallupa Bullee) has, for two months, fed about 1,000 people daily at his own expense.’

Extract paragraphs 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5, of Mr. Hearn's report No. 33, dated 11th September 1866, to the address of the Collector of Dharwar.

“ ‘1. With reference to your endorsement No. 1158, dated 13th ultimo, I have the honor to submit my opinion and report as to the best works to be undertaken in the Nuwulgoond and Roan Talookas for the relief of the sufferers among the poorer classes from the present scarcity of grain and other causes.

“ ‘2. You are probably aware that there has been a large influx of people into these and other Talookas of the Dharwar Collectorate from the neighbouring Collectorates of Bellary, Kulladghee, and Belgaum. The people from Bellary have left it principally on account of the famine prices prevailing there: those from Kulladgee and Belgaum have been driven here, as I am informed, by the high prices of grain ruling there, as well as the ravages of cholera (which has since abated) in some of the villages bordering on the Dharwar Collectorate.

“ ‘3. During my journey from Dharwar to Nuwulgoond on the 3rd instant I met a great number of people (inhabitants of this and the surrounding districts) proceeding towards Dharwar, who, with scarcely any exception, stated they were driven to do so to seek employment as they were suffering from want, and I need hardly [p. 110] observe that their wretched appearance fully bore out what they said. In one or two instances the parties were so weak, evidently from hunger, that they were obliged to lie down to rest themselves. From the enquiries which I have verbally made I have reason to believe that large numbers of our villages have, from their impoverished circumstances, been obliged to content themselves with one scanty meal a day.

“ ‘4. All the tanks and some of the wells which I inspected since leaving Dharwar I found completely dry; the villagers being thus obliged to resort to Nullahs and holes several miles away, the supply of water in which being small occupied a great deal of time in collection and conveyance. It will thus be seen that the poor are suffering from the want of the very necessaries of life, viz., food and water.

“ ‘5. On the last market days at Nuwulgoond and Nurgoond grain was sold at the following rates :—

“ ‘ NuwulgoondRice (coarse) at 3 seers per Rupee.Jowaree at 3½ per Rupee.
“ ‘ NurgoondRice (coarse) at 3 seers per Rupee.Jowaree at 3½ per Rupee.’

“ 6. The following memorandum recorded in 1866 by the then Acting llector, Mr. Shaw Stewart, details so fully the arrangements made for eviating the distress that prevailed that the Collector begs to be excused quoting it at length.

Dharwar Collector’s Office, 20th September 1866.

“ ‘ 1. It is necessary to lay down some instructions for the guidance of District Officers if the present scarcity and distress lasts longer. It is evident that unless rain falls generally within ten days throughout those portions of the Collectorate where dry crops are grown the coming harvest will be a failure ; and even if rain falls there is no doubt, from the reports I have received, that the chance of a fair crop is in many places already gone, while every day’s delay in the fall of rain adds to the extent of failure.

“ ‘ 2. This is especially to be regretted because of the small quantity of grain that is now in store in the district, in consequence of the demands made on the grain reserves during the last three seasons of deficient harvests; and it seems to be generally considered that even if the grain in stores were made available there would not be enough to supply food for the population till the harvest of 1867-68.

[p. 111]

“ ‘ 3. There is thus every prospect of the present high prices continuing unabated for a lengthened period, and they may even become still more exorbitant. Distress and suffering will thus become greater.

“ ‘ 4. Under these circumstances, the first thing that the Officers of Government have to do is to endeavour to find employment for the unemployed poor, and the second thing is to provide against the contingency of the supplies of food becoming exhausted.

“ ‘ 5. As regards employment, the Acting Executive Engineer and I have, by the permission of the Revenue Commissioner, directed the commencement of various large public works in the Talookas where the distress has been greatest, on which works it is calculated that about Rs. 2,000 will be spent every day till the end of this month, and this will undoubtedly relieve many from pressure of want and famine.

“ ‘ 6. But it is necessary to look forward to the future, and to be prepared to find employment for the poor so long as the present high prices prevail.

“ ‘ 7. The kinds of works that are most desirable to undertake for this purpose are those that give employment to the greatest number of persons, men, women and children, where no great professional skill is required, and which have for their object the improvement of water supply, or traffic communications. No work is so useful and appropriate as excavating and removing earth from large tanks or preparing metal for roads; but there are others that can be advantageously carried on, such as road-making, digging holes on the road sides for trees, or digging wells.

“ ‘ 8. I wish all the Mamludars, Mahalkurees and Foujdars, to look at the matter with a view to the future, and be prepared whenever there are many unemployed poor persons in any place to propose some useful work on which to employ them. I will approve and sanction any such work being commenced by the Mamludars whenever the emergency is such that neither my own nor the Assistant Collector’s orders can be waited for. This may happen in the case of a gang of poor people coming unexpectedly from other districts, but ought not to be necessary if precaution is used. Generally speaking, the orders of the Collector, or Assistant Collector, should be asked for before any new work is commenced, and in the event of any such application being received by an Assistant Collector he will exercise his discretion in ordering its commencement at once, or refer to me for orders. In the event of his ordering such a work to be begun at once, the Mamludar is desired and empowered to make a daily payment of wages out of the [p. 112] Treasury till such time as the Hoozoor orders are received. All such proceedings should be fully reported to the Hoozoor without loss of time.

“ ‘9. It is very important that all wandering gangs should be stopped and employed on work. I beg that this may be carefully attended to, and that no person be allowed to wander about the country in search of food, or alms, or work.

“ ‘10. The Shetsundees are reported to suffer much from distress at this time: for this purpose such of them as can be spared from the necessary duties in their villages ought to be employed in connection with these works as overseers of gangs, or to guard tools, or stores, or treasure, and receive proper wages for this duty, independently of their official emoluments.

“ ‘11. Where large bodies of labourers are collected at any work great care should be used to see that the ground near them is kept clean, and that they get proper water and food. All precautions must be taken to prevent disease breaking out among them, and on these duties the Shetsundees might be employed.

“ ‘12. A small percentage of the outlay, not exceeding two per cent., may be spent on extra establishment for supervision and accounts.

“ ‘13. With regard to preparing metal, I mention the following roads on the sides of which large heaps of metal may be collected—

  • 1. Teygoor to Hurreeher throughout.
  • 2. Hooblee to Koonoor, at Hooblee and Nurgoond, and any other places where stone is procurable.
  • 3. Hooblee to Gudug, at Koosoogul, Annigherry and Gudug, and any other places where stone is procurable.
  • 4. Dharwar to Hullial.
  • 5. Hooblee to Sungtteecope.
  • 6. Bunkapoor to Moondgode.

Detailed instructions will be issued by the Executive Engineer and by me for this work; in the mean time I only mention the places and roads where large heaps of metal may be formed.

“ ‘14. For these works I think that for the present 3 annas should be the highest rate of wages for an ordinary labourer, 2½ for a woman, and 2 or 1½ for a child; and I should prefer that the day’s wages should be paid in cash at the end of each day, as the trouble of mustering will be avoided. It is not necessary to pay each person [p. 113] separately our supply of copper would run short, but a family or party might be paid in silver through the head-man. I shall, however, be glad to receive any suggestions for altering these rules of pay and the method of payment. Perhaps in some cases grain payments may be necessary, but I think that it is better, if possible, to provide a few Bunyas' shops near each large work, and to let the people buy food with their wages, due encouragement and protection being given to the Bunyas.

“ ‘15. The second matter referred to above will, perhaps, prove to be more important than the supply of work, namely, the supply of food, and I beg all the Mamludars will turn their attention to the subject, and consider whether there is enough grain in the district to supply the population with food if this harvest should fail partially or wholly; and if there is not enough that they will consider and ascertain whether it can be procured from the neighbouring districts, or whether supplies are likely to flow in by the ordinary action of traders. On this important point I would like to receive a report, as soon as possible, from each Mamludar.

“ ‘16. Regarding the old and infirm, and those not able to work for a livelihood, I acknowledge with great pleasure the humane and liberal charity of which I hear accounts from all quarters, and I have such confidence in the people of this Zilla that I know the old and infirm will never be left to starve. Still, if this distress continues long, the demands on the private charity of the rich may exceed their means of meeting it, and I therefore wish the Mamludars to let me know whether they think any aid is required from elsewhere.

“ ‘17. Till further orders, I request that a short report may be sent to me direct every Saturday by each Mamludar and Mahalkuree of the state of his Talooka, and the progress of works and the prospects of the crops.’

“7. The following table shows the rates of grain sold at Dharwar uring the distress :—

[p. 114]

“ 8. The prices now prevailing are as shown below :—

“ 9. The harvest has been the best reaped for several seasons, and, owing to less land having been taken up for cotton, more cereals were cultivated, and the supply is now abundant.

“ 10. It may suffice to mention that the distress quite disappeared by December.

“ 11. The Collector has much pleasure of the opportunity of stating that, in addition to the aid afforded by Government in affording employment to the unemployed and poor, much private benevolence was displayed, wherever the distress prevailed, in the support of the young, old and infirm. The charitable exertions of the wealthier Natives and of entire communities were very timely, and the subjoined statement shows the extent to which charity was extended:—

"12. In conclusion, the Collector feels much satisfaction in stating that while the distress prevailed subscriptions were collected for the relief of the sufferers on the East Coast of India, and though under the sanction contained in Government Resolution No. 3568, dated 22nd September 1866, some of the funds collected were diverted for the relief of local distress, a remittance of over 3,700 Rupees has been made to the Secretaries in Bombay of the Bengal Famine Relief Fund."

54. This completes the famine reports of the several Officers in charge of tracts of country above the Ghauts from the northern-most to the southern-most extremity of the Presidency. I will now proceed to record the statements of the few Collectors in charge of districts in the Concan, below the Ghauts, and between the Ghauts and the sea-board of the Western Coast.

55. On 5th October the Acting Collector of Tannah, Mr. Arbuthnot, replied to my reference as follows :—

"1. With reference to your Circular dated 1st July last, I have the honor to report that there are no records here to show the occurrence of any famine in this Collectorate.

"2. From enquiries made from the old inhabitants it appears that a famine occurred in the year 1802 or 1803 in the up-country, probably in the district bordering on the Godavery River, called Gungthudee, when hundreds of famished people came into the Concan, and were fed by the private charity of the inhabitants. It had the effect of raising the prices of grain in these parts. Government assisted, it is said, by employing these men on the work of the Bombay and Tannah road then under construction. These labourers were allowed each a seer, or twenty-eight tolas, of rice and an old pice. Government also established a shop at Oorun, and rice brought from Bombay was sold there at one pailee a Rupee.

"3. In 1824-25, owing to scanty rains, the crop yielded in Dhanoo, Bassein, and Salsette Talookas one-fourth the usual produce, and consequently great scarcity of grain prevailed. Rice and other grains were sold at famine prices. No casualties, however, occurred. The only assistance Government gave was the exemption from assessment, but there is no record to verify this.

"4. In 1838-39 also, owing to the failure of rain in Salsette, the crops failed, but fortunately by a timely arrival of a supply of rice from Malabar the population was saved from the impending calamities."

[p. 117]

56. I suggested to the Acting Collector that further minute enquiries should be made during the following three months, and that the result should be communicated at the end of that time.

57. On 21st January last I received a further communication from the Tannah Acting Collector, Mr. Robertson, to the effect that further minute enquiry had elicited no additional information—that the few records which were forthcoming contained no notice whatever of famines or scarcities.

58. On 6th September Mr. Salmon, the Acting Sub-Collector of Colaba, replied that—

“ 2. There are no written records of any famine in the Sub-Collectorate, and there does not appear to have been such a serious scarcity of food since the introduction of the British rule as could properly be termed a famine. Considerable remissions to the extent of one-sixth to one-tenth of the revenue were granted in 1817-18 in the parts of the Sub-Collectorate under British rule, but no extraordinary measures of relief seem to have been called for, or resorted to, on the part of Government. Charitable Natives distributed food, but the scarcity only lasted a year, and was attended with no loss of life.

“ 3. All over the Sub-Collectorate there is oral evidence of a great famine in the early part of the present century between 1801—1804. This extended over the Deccan from Malabar up to Guzerat. Commencing in the country above the Ghauts, the influx of people in search of food from there into the Concan, added to a scarcity of rain, and consequent partial failure of crops, caused the extension of this famine to these districts. All the oral information given states this to have been a very severe famine. Great numbers are said to have died, and children to have been sold for food.
